I was just standing there holding the key to the cash drawer, and instead of actually going to the register and closing out my checks like I was supposed to, I was just staring at the syrup boxes and repeating my table numbers out loud. Over and over. \"Table four, table seven, table twelve.\" Just like that. \n\nAnd my coworker had to come shake my shoulder because I guess I'd been doing it for a few minutes and the customers were waiting and the checks were just piling up. It's just... it's not like me to mess up the flow like that, but my body just stops, or my brain gets stuck on the numbers, and I can't get to the next step of what I'm supposed to be doing. They told me I needed to get checked out before I could come back on shift because it really freaked out the kitchen staff, too.\n\nDoctor: When you were standing at the soda station repeating the table numbers, were you aware of what was happening around you at that moment, or is it kind of a blank—like you don't remember what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I blacked out or anything. I wasn't unconscious, and it wasn't like a dream where you wake up and have no idea how you got there. I knew I was at the soda station. I could feel the cold air coming off the ice machine, and I could hear the clinking of the plates in the kitchen, and I knew the syrup boxes were right in front of me. The boxes were the ones with the little red labels, the cherry flavor, which we always run out of so fast, and I remember thinking about how we needed to change the bag soon. \n\nSo I was aware, yeah. I could hear the ambient noise of the diner, the low hum of people talking, the sizzle from the grill—Jimmy was working the grill that day, and he always makes this specific scraping sound with his spatula. But it was like all of that was happening on the other side of a thick glass window, you know? Like I was trapped in this little bubble where the only thing that mattered, the only thing that was real, was the table numbers. \"Table four, table seven, table twelve.\" \n\nI heard myself saying them, and I knew I was saying them out loud, but I just couldn't make myself stop and walk over to the register. It was like the numbers were the most important thing in the world, and if I didn't keep repeating them, something terrible would happen, or I'd just lose them forever. And I knew my hands were holding the key, the little brass key with the blue plastic tag that we use for the drawer, and I could feel the edges of it pressing into my palm, but my legs just wouldn't move. I was just stuck there, fully awake, fully aware that I was stuck, but completely unable to break out of the loop until Sarah—she's the coworker who came over—until she actually grabbed my shoulder and physically jolted me out of it.\n\nDoctor: When you were repeating those table numbers, did it feel like you absolutely had to do it because you were worried something bad would happen if you didn't, or did it feel more like your mind was just stuck on autopilot and you couldn't redirect your attention away from the numbers?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it’s definitely more like the autopilot thing, but... like a broken autopilot? Because, like I was trying to explain earlier, that’s exactly what scared everyone at work so much. It wasn’t that I was having some intense worry about the world ending or something terrible happening if I didn't say the numbers. It was just this total, complete inability to move to the next logical step. \n\nI just started freezing up right in the middle of service, you know? And doing things completely out of order. Like, I’ve got the cash drawer key right there in my hand. The only thing you do with that key is walk to the register, put it in, turn it, and close out the checks. That’s it. It’s a simple sequence, one I've done literally thousands of times since I got hired. But instead, my brain just got stuck on that one track, and I’m just standing there by the soda station, staring at the syrups, repeating those table numbers over and over instead of actually closing the checks. \n\nIt’s like the connection between knowing what I’m supposed to do and actually doing it just completely vanished. So I’m just frozen there, totally out of sequence, holding this key like it’s just some useless piece of metal, while the checks are just sitting there waiting to be closed. And I couldn't redirect my attention at all. No matter how much I knew I needed to go to the register, my mouth just kept going with the numbers. Which, you know, when you're in the middle of a lunch rush and the kitchen is backed up and everyone is relying on you to keep the flow going, freezing up like that and doing things so out of order is just... well, it's a disaster, really. It just makes no sense to my brain why I can't just walk over and use the key.\n\nDoctor: When you were repeating those table numbers, did it feel like something inside you was *pushing* you to keep saying them—like an urge or a compulsion you had to obey—or did it feel more like your body and mind just wouldn't respond to what you wanted to do, like they were stuck?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was definitely the second one. Like I was saying, it wasn't an urge at all. I didn't feel any kind of... push, or like I was being forced to obey some rule or anything like that. I know I keep coming back to this one specific incident, but it's just that this is exactly what scared everyone at work so badly, you know? Because I was just... stuck. Completely stuck. I wanted to move. I knew I needed to move. But I was just freezing up right in the middle of service. \n\nIt’s like my brain just forgot how to send the signals to my legs. I’m standing there by the soda station, which is right in the main walkway between the kitchen doors and the dining room, so literally everyone can see me, and I’m holding that cash drawer key. The logical next step, the thing I actively wanted to do, was to walk up to the front counter—it's right past the pie display—and close out the checks for those tables. That's the normal routine. But instead of closing the checks, I'm doing things completely out of order. I'm just frozen there, staring straight ahead at the syrups, and my mouth is just running on a loop, repeating those table numbers. \n\nI wasn't doing it because I felt a compulsion to do it. It was more like... I don't know, like a computer freezing on a single screen, and the audio just keeps skipping on the exact same word over and over. I wanted to break out of it, I wanted to just take the key and go to the register, but my body and mind just absolutely would not respond to what I was trying to make them do. And that’s why my manager and the kitchen staff were so freaked out, because standing by the soda machine reciting numbers instead of doing my job... it just doesn't make any sense. I was trapped in the glitch, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Before you started repeating those table numbers, did you notice any thoughts or worries going through your mind that felt intrusive or hard to push away—like thoughts that kept popping back into your head even though you didn't want them to? Or did the repetition of the numbers just start happening without any particular thoughts leading it?\n\nPatient: No, there really wasn't anything like that. I wasn't having any bad thoughts, or, you know, worries that I couldn't push away or anything. Honestly, right before it happened, my mind was just doing the normal diner calculus. It was the middle of the lunch rush, so I was just mentally mapping out my next steps, which is something you have to do when you're managing a whole section by yourself. \n\nI remember I had just left table twelve—they were this nice older couple who come in a lot, and they wanted the meatloaf special, but he's very particular about the gravy being on the side, so I was making a mental note to make sure Jimmy didn't smother it. And then table seven had just flagged me down for iced tea refills, and table four was ready to pay. So I was just organizing that sequence in my head. You know, get the check closed out for four, grab the tea pitcher for seven, put the ticket in for twelve. It’s just waitress math. My brain was completely focused on the logistics of the floor, just keeping the rhythm going. \n\nThere were no weird thoughts popping up, no anxiety about something going wrong, no intrusive worries at all. I was just doing my job. I walked over to the soda station because the extra tea pitchers are kept right next to the ice bin, and I was just reaching out to grab the handle—it’s this heavy plastic pitcher that always gets condensation all over the outside so you have to grip it tight—and that’s when the glitch just... happened. It just started out of nowhere. One second I'm thinking about iced tea and gravy, and the very next second, my brain just dropped everything else and the numbers took over and I was frozen. There was no build-up or warning or any specific thought leading into it at all, it just hijacked me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this freezing up has happened a few times at work. In those other episodes when you froze up, did you always find yourself repeating something out loud like you did with the table numbers, or have there been times when you just couldn't move or couldn't get your body to do what you wanted it to do without any repetitive behavior happening?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it’s not always the talking. I know I’ve been talking your ear off about that one specific time with the table numbers today, but I just want to make sure you really understand the core of why it was such a big deal. Because what genuinely scared everyone at work wasn't necessarily just the repeating out loud part—it's the fact that I started freezing up right in the middle of service. Period. Just stopping dead in my tracks. Or doing things completely out of order, which, in a restaurant, is basically a cardinal sin, you know? \n\nYou have a system, you follow the system. Everything is about timing and sequence. So when I break that system, people notice immediately. Like, I know I already brought up standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key instead of actually going to the register and closing out the checks, but that’s just the absolute perfect example of doing things out of order. The repeating table numbers part of that specific incident was terrifying, sure, but there have definitely been other times where I just freeze and I'm completely silent. \n\nI won't be saying a single word. I'll just be standing there, totally stuck, maybe holding a tray or a coffee pot or even that same cash drawer key, and my body just won't go. It's the freezing and the out-of-sequence actions that really throw everyone off. Because one minute I'm fine, walking through my section, and the next minute I'm just a statue in the middle of the main aisle, not doing the very obvious next thing I'm supposed to be doing. And the whole kitchen staff and my manager are just staring at me, wondering why I'm not moving when there are tickets piling up. So, yeah, to answer your question, the verbal repeating part isn't always there, but the freezing up and the doing things out of order... that's the constant. That's the part that really shook up the staff.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up like that, is it always something that just happens to you without warning, or have you noticed that it tends to happen more when you're feeling stressed or anxious about something specific—either at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, it really isn’t about being stressed or anxious. I mean, sure, working in a diner during the lunch rush is inherently stressful, right? You’ve got plates clattering, the line cooks are yelling because they need hands to run food, people are waving their empty coffee mugs at you, the phone is ringing for takeout orders... it’s a high-pressure environment. But that’s just the job. I know how to handle a rush. It’s a good kind of stress, usually, like a workout. And my personal life is pretty quiet right now, there’s no big drama with my family or anything keeping me up at night. So it’s not like I’m standing on the floor worrying about my bills or having a panic attack about my life. It really just happens completely without warning. \n\nI know I keep circling back to this, and I probably sound like a broken record today, but I just really need to make sure you see the whole picture of why this was such a massive deal to the people I work with. Because what actually scared everyone at work so much wasn't that I was acting stressed out or overwhelmed—it was that I started freezing up right in the absolute middle of service, or just doing things completely out of order for no logical reason. \n\nLike that incident I keep talking about... standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and just repeating those table numbers over and over instead of actually going up to the front and closing out the checks. If I was just stressed, I might snap at a busboy or, I don't know, forget a side of ranch or drop a plate. But freezing up and doing my routine entirely out of order like that? Standing by the syrups with the register key instead of cashing people out? That’s not anxiety. That’s just... my brain completely losing the plot without any warning whatsoever. And that’s why my manager and Jimmy in the kitchen looked at me like I had two heads, because I wasn't acting frantic or anxious, I was just completely broken out of the normal sequence of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up like that, are you able to move other parts of your body—like your arms or your head—or does your entire body just go completely rigid and locked in place?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s… it’s like my whole body just turns to concrete. I really can’t move my arms or even just turn my head to look at someone if they yell my name. I’m completely locked in place. And I know, I know I’ve brought this specific example up like five times already today, and I'm sorry to keep harping on it, but it’s just… it’s the exact reason why everyone at the diner got so incredibly scared. Because it’s not just that I get a little stiff or need a second to catch my breath, it’s the freezing up right in the absolute middle of a crazy service, and doing everything completely out of order. \n\nLike, when I was stuck there by the soda station, my arm was just… suspended. I was holding that cash drawer key, and my fingers were clamped around it so tight my knuckles were probably white, but I couldn't lower my arm or walk to the front. I couldn't make my legs move toward the register to actually close out the checks like I was supposed to. I was just a statue. The only thing moving was my mouth, just repeating those table numbers over and over, while the rest of me was just totally rigid. I couldn't even turn my neck to look at my manager when he came over to see what was wrong. So, yeah, it’s the whole body. It just locks up completely, and I'm stuck there doing things entirely out of sequence instead of my actual job.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when your whole body freezes up, are you completely conscious and aware of everything happening around you the whole time, or do you lose consciousness or have gaps in your memory of what happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, I am completely awake. That’s… honestly, that’s the absolute worst part of the whole thing. I don't black out at all, not even for a second. I remember every single detail. I can hear the plates clattering in the bus tubs, I can hear the bell dinging in the window when food is up, I can smell the fryer grease... my brain is just sitting there inside my head watching everything happen, but I just can't do anything about it. \n\nAnd I know, look, I know I keep coming back to this same point, and I swear I'm not trying to beat a dead horse today, but the fact that I am totally awake and aware is exactly what scared everyone at work so badly. Because they could look at my face and see that my eyes were open and tracking things while I started freezing up right in the middle of service, or doing things entirely out of order. They could tell I wasn't fainting or having a seizure where I was checked out. \n\nLike, during that time I keep talking about, when I was standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key? I knew I was holding it. I knew exactly what it was for, and I knew in my head that I was supposed to be walking up to the front register to actually close out those checks. But instead, I'm just planted there, repeating those table numbers out loud over and over, and I'm fully, one-hundred-percent aware that I'm doing it instead of closing the checks! I could literally see my manager walking over looking incredibly confused, I could hear a customer in my section asking for a side of mayo, but I just couldn't stop the repeating or make my legs move away from the ice machine. \n\nSo, no, there are absolutely no gaps in my memory. None. I just have to stand there, completely trapped inside my own body, knowing that I'm breaking the entire flow of the restaurant and terrifying my coworkers because I'm not doing the next logical step in my job. It’s just… it’s really exhausting to remember it all so clearly, to be perfectly honest with you, because I know exactly how bizarre it looks to everyone else while it's happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean... I’ve tried to figure that out, honestly. I’ve racked my brain trying to see if there’s some sort of common denominator, like maybe it only happens when I’m dealing with a difficult customer at booth four, or maybe it’s when I’m carrying the heavy bus tubs back to the dish pit. Because what scared everyone at work so much wasn't *where* I was standing, it was the fact that I started freezing up right in the middle of service and just doing things completely, entirely out of order. 

If there was a pattern, maybe it would make sense. But there wasn't. Like with that incident... the cash register is all the way up at the front host stand, right by the front door. But I was standing all the way over by the soda station. I was holding the cash drawer key, which meant my brain knew I was supposed to be up front closing out checks for my tables. But instead of doing that, instead of following the normal sequence of my job, I just got stuck by the ice machine, repeating those table numbers over and over again. It didn't make any sense for me to be doing that right then, or in that specific spot. I wasn't triggered by the soda machine, you know? It just... happened. It just hijacked me right there, completely out of order from what I was actually supposed to be doing, and that complete randomness is exactly why my manager was so freaked out by it.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Um, well, I guess my manager is the one who really pushed for this. I work as a waitress at this diner, and usually, I'm really good at my job. I went to college for a couple of years, so I'm smart, and I've been serving for a while now, and you know how it gets during a lunch rush—it's just go, go, go. You have to keep a million things in your head at once, the drinks, the modifications, who needs ranch. But lately... well, what really scared everyone at work, and honestly it kind of freaked me out too, is that I just... I started freezing up in the middle of service. \n\nLike, right in the middle of the floor. Or I'd start doing things completely out of order, which makes no sense because I know the routine backward and forward. The other day, my manager found me just standing over by the soda station. I was just standing there holding the key to the cash drawer, and instead of actually going to the register and closing out my checks like I was supposed to, I was just staring at the syrup boxes and repeating my table numbers out loud. Over and over. \"Table four, table seven, table twelve.\" Just like that. \n\nAnd my coworker had to come shake my shoulder because I guess I'd been doing it for a few minutes and the customers were waiting and the checks were just piling up. It's just... it's not like me to mess up the flow like that, but my body just stops, or my brain gets stuck on the numbers, and I can't get to the next step of what I'm supposed to be doing. They told me I needed to get checked out before I could come back on shift because it really freaked out the kitchen staff, too.\n\nDoctor: When you were standing at the soda station repeating the table numbers, were you aware of what was happening around you at that moment, or is it kind of a blank—like you don't remember what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I blacked out or anything. I wasn't unconscious, and it wasn't like a dream where you wake up and have no idea how you got there. I knew I was at the soda station. I could feel the cold air coming off the ice machine, and I could hear the clinking of the plates in the kitchen, and I knew the syrup boxes were right in front of me. The boxes were the ones with the little red labels, the cherry flavor, which we always run out of so fast, and I remember thinking about how we needed to change the bag soon. \n\nSo I was aware, yeah. I could hear the ambient noise of the diner, the low hum of people talking, the sizzle from the grill—Jimmy was working the grill that day, and he always makes this specific scraping sound with his spatula. But it was like all of that was happening on the other side of a thick glass window, you know? Like I was trapped in this little bubble where the only thing that mattered, the only thing that was real, was the table numbers. \"Table four, table seven, table twelve.\" \n\nI heard myself saying them, and I knew I was saying them out loud, but I just couldn't make myself stop and walk over to the register. It was like the numbers were the most important thing in the world, and if I didn't keep repeating them, something terrible would happen, or I'd just lose them forever. And I knew my hands were holding the key, the little brass key with the blue plastic tag that we use for the drawer, and I could feel the edges of it pressing into my palm, but my legs just wouldn't move. I was just stuck there, fully awake, fully aware that I was stuck, but completely unable to break out of the loop until Sarah—she's the coworker who came over—until she actually grabbed my shoulder and physically jolted me out of it.\n\nDoctor: When you were repeating those table numbers, did it feel like you absolutely had to do it because you were worried something bad would happen if you didn't, or did it feel more like your mind was just stuck on autopilot and you couldn't redirect your attention away from the numbers?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it’s definitely more like the autopilot thing, but... like a broken autopilot? Because, like I was trying to explain earlier, that’s exactly what scared everyone at work so much. It wasn’t that I was having some intense worry about the world ending or something terrible happening if I didn't say the numbers. It was just this total, complete inability to move to the next logical step. \n\nI just started freezing up right in the middle of service, you know? And doing things completely out of order. Like, I’ve got the cash drawer key right there in my hand. The only thing you do with that key is walk to the register, put it in, turn it, and close out the checks. That’s it. It’s a simple sequence, one I've done literally thousands of times since I got hired. But instead, my brain just got stuck on that one track, and I’m just standing there by the soda station, staring at the syrups, repeating those table numbers over and over instead of actually closing the checks. \n\nIt’s like the connection between knowing what I’m supposed to do and actually doing it just completely vanished. So I’m just frozen there, totally out of sequence, holding this key like it’s just some useless piece of metal, while the checks are just sitting there waiting to be closed. And I couldn't redirect my attention at all. No matter how much I knew I needed to go to the register, my mouth just kept going with the numbers. Which, you know, when you're in the middle of a lunch rush and the kitchen is backed up and everyone is relying on you to keep the flow going, freezing up like that and doing things so out of order is just... well, it's a disaster, really. It just makes no sense to my brain why I can't just walk over and use the key.\n\nDoctor: When you were repeating those table numbers, did it feel like something inside you was *pushing* you to keep saying them—like an urge or a compulsion you had to obey—or did it feel more like your body and mind just wouldn't respond to what you wanted to do, like they were stuck?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was definitely the second one. Like I was saying, it wasn't an urge at all. I didn't feel any kind of... push, or like I was being forced to obey some rule or anything like that. I know I keep coming back to this one specific incident, but it's just that this is exactly what scared everyone at work so badly, you know? Because I was just... stuck. Completely stuck. I wanted to move. I knew I needed to move. But I was just freezing up right in the middle of service. \n\nIt’s like my brain just forgot how to send the signals to my legs. I’m standing there by the soda station, which is right in the main walkway between the kitchen doors and the dining room, so literally everyone can see me, and I’m holding that cash drawer key. The logical next step, the thing I actively wanted to do, was to walk up to the front counter—it's right past the pie display—and close out the checks for those tables. That's the normal routine. But instead of closing the checks, I'm doing things completely out of order. I'm just frozen there, staring straight ahead at the syrups, and my mouth is just running on a loop, repeating those table numbers. \n\nI wasn't doing it because I felt a compulsion to do it. It was more like... I don't know, like a computer freezing on a single screen, and the audio just keeps skipping on the exact same word over and over. I wanted to break out of it, I wanted to just take the key and go to the register, but my body and mind just absolutely would not respond to what I was trying to make them do. And that’s why my manager and the kitchen staff were so freaked out, because standing by the soda machine reciting numbers instead of doing my job... it just doesn't make any sense. I was trapped in the glitch, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Before you started repeating those table numbers, did you notice any thoughts or worries going through your mind that felt intrusive or hard to push away—like thoughts that kept popping back into your head even though you didn't want them to? Or did the repetition of the numbers just start happening without any particular thoughts leading it?\n\nPatient: No, there really wasn't anything like that. I wasn't having any bad thoughts, or, you know, worries that I couldn't push away or anything. Honestly, right before it happened, my mind was just doing the normal diner calculus. It was the middle of the lunch rush, so I was just mentally mapping out my next steps, which is something you have to do when you're managing a whole section by yourself. \n\nI remember I had just left table twelve—they were this nice older couple who come in a lot, and they wanted the meatloaf special, but he's very particular about the gravy being on the side, so I was making a mental note to make sure Jimmy didn't smother it. And then table seven had just flagged me down for iced tea refills, and table four was ready to pay. So I was just organizing that sequence in my head. You know, get the check closed out for four, grab the tea pitcher for seven, put the ticket in for twelve. It’s just waitress math. My brain was completely focused on the logistics of the floor, just keeping the rhythm going. \n\nThere were no weird thoughts popping up, no anxiety about something going wrong, no intrusive worries at all. I was just doing my job. I walked over to the soda station because the extra tea pitchers are kept right next to the ice bin, and I was just reaching out to grab the handle—it’s this heavy plastic pitcher that always gets condensation all over the outside so you have to grip it tight—and that’s when the glitch just... happened. It just started out of nowhere. One second I'm thinking about iced tea and gravy, and the very next second, my brain just dropped everything else and the numbers took over and I was frozen. There was no build-up or warning or any specific thought leading into it at all, it just hijacked me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this freezing up has happened a few times at work. In those other episodes when you froze up, did you always find yourself repeating something out loud like you did with the table numbers, or have there been times when you just couldn't move or couldn't get your body to do what you wanted it to do without any repetitive behavior happening?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it’s not always the talking. I know I’ve been talking your ear off about that one specific time with the table numbers today, but I just want to make sure you really understand the core of why it was such a big deal. Because what genuinely scared everyone at work wasn't necessarily just the repeating out loud part—it's the fact that I started freezing up right in the middle of service. Period. Just stopping dead in my tracks. Or doing things completely out of order, which, in a restaurant, is basically a cardinal sin, you know? \n\nYou have a system, you follow the system. Everything is about timing and sequence. So when I break that system, people notice immediately. Like, I know I already brought up standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key instead of actually going to the register and closing out the checks, but that’s just the absolute perfect example of doing things out of order. The repeating table numbers part of that specific incident was terrifying, sure, but there have definitely been other times where I just freeze and I'm completely silent. \n\nI won't be saying a single word. I'll just be standing there, totally stuck, maybe holding a tray or a coffee pot or even that same cash drawer key, and my body just won't go. It's the freezing and the out-of-sequence actions that really throw everyone off. Because one minute I'm fine, walking through my section, and the next minute I'm just a statue in the middle of the main aisle, not doing the very obvious next thing I'm supposed to be doing. And the whole kitchen staff and my manager are just staring at me, wondering why I'm not moving when there are tickets piling up. So, yeah, to answer your question, the verbal repeating part isn't always there, but the freezing up and the doing things out of order... that's the constant. That's the part that really shook up the staff.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up like that, is it always something that just happens to you without warning, or have you noticed that it tends to happen more when you're feeling stressed or anxious about something specific—either at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, it really isn’t about being stressed or anxious. I mean, sure, working in a diner during the lunch rush is inherently stressful, right? You’ve got plates clattering, the line cooks are yelling because they need hands to run food, people are waving their empty coffee mugs at you, the phone is ringing for takeout orders... it’s a high-pressure environment. But that’s just the job. I know how to handle a rush. It’s a good kind of stress, usually, like a workout. And my personal life is pretty quiet right now, there’s no big drama with my family or anything keeping me up at night. So it’s not like I’m standing on the floor worrying about my bills or having a panic attack about my life. It really just happens completely without warning. \n\nI know I keep circling back to this, and I probably sound like a broken record today, but I just really need to make sure you see the whole picture of why this was such a massive deal to the people I work with. Because what actually scared everyone at work so much wasn't that I was acting stressed out or overwhelmed—it was that I started freezing up right in the absolute middle of service, or just doing things completely out of order for no logical reason. \n\nLike that incident I keep talking about... standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and just repeating those table numbers over and over instead of actually going up to the front and closing out the checks. If I was just stressed, I might snap at a busboy or, I don't know, forget a side of ranch or drop a plate. But freezing up and doing my routine entirely out of order like that? Standing by the syrups with the register key instead of cashing people out? That’s not anxiety. That’s just... my brain completely losing the plot without any warning whatsoever. And that’s why my manager and Jimmy in the kitchen looked at me like I had two heads, because I wasn't acting frantic or anxious, I was just completely broken out of the normal sequence of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up like that, are you able to move other parts of your body—like your arms or your head—or does your entire body just go completely rigid and locked in place?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s… it’s like my whole body just turns to concrete. I really can’t move my arms or even just turn my head to look at someone if they yell my name. I’m completely locked in place. And I know, I know I’ve brought this specific example up like five times already today, and I'm sorry to keep harping on it, but it’s just… it’s the exact reason why everyone at the diner got so incredibly scared. Because it’s not just that I get a little stiff or need a second to catch my breath, it’s the freezing up right in the absolute middle of a crazy service, and doing everything completely out of order. \n\nLike, when I was stuck there by the soda station, my arm was just… suspended. I was holding that cash drawer key, and my fingers were clamped around it so tight my knuckles were probably white, but I couldn't lower my arm or walk to the front. I couldn't make my legs move toward the register to actually close out the checks like I was supposed to. I was just a statue. The only thing moving was my mouth, just repeating those table numbers over and over, while the rest of me was just totally rigid. I couldn't even turn my neck to look at my manager when he came over to see what was wrong. So, yeah, it’s the whole body. It just locks up completely, and I'm stuck there doing things entirely out of sequence instead of my actual job.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when your whole body freezes up, are you completely conscious and aware of everything happening around you the whole time, or do you lose consciousness or have gaps in your memory of what happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, I am completely awake. That’s… honestly, that’s the absolute worst part of the whole thing. I don't black out at all, not even for a second. I remember every single detail. I can hear the plates clattering in the bus tubs, I can hear the bell dinging in the window when food is up, I can smell the fryer grease... my brain is just sitting there inside my head watching everything happen, but I just can't do anything about it. \n\nAnd I know, look, I know I keep coming back to this same point, and I swear I'm not trying to beat a dead horse today, but the fact that I am totally awake and aware is exactly what scared everyone at work so badly. Because they could look at my face and see that my eyes were open and tracking things while I started freezing up right in the middle of service, or doing things entirely out of order. They could tell I wasn't fainting or having a seizure where I was checked out. \n\nLike, during that time I keep talking about, when I was standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key? I knew I was holding it. I knew exactly what it was for, and I knew in my head that I was supposed to be walking up to the front register to actually close out those checks. But instead, I'm just planted there, repeating those table numbers out loud over and over, and I'm fully, one-hundred-percent aware that I'm doing it instead of closing the checks! I could literally see my manager walking over looking incredibly confused, I could hear a customer in my section asking for a side of mayo, but I just couldn't stop the repeating or make my legs move away from the ice machine. \n\nSo, no, there are absolutely no gaps in my memory. None. I just have to stand there, completely trapped inside my own body, knowing that I'm breaking the entire flow of the restaurant and terrifying my coworkers because I'm not doing the next logical step in my job. It’s just… it’s really exhausting to remember it all so clearly, to be perfectly honest with you, because I know exactly how bizarre it looks to everyone else while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at all the times you've frozen up at work, have you noticed any kind of pattern—like it happens more when you're in a particular area of the diner, or when you're doing a specific task, or around certain people? Or does it really just happen completely randomly no matter what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: I mean... I’ve tried to figure that out, honestly. I’ve racked my brain trying to see if there’s some sort of common denominator, like maybe it only happens when I’m dealing with a difficult customer at booth four, or maybe it’s when I’m carrying the heavy bus tubs back to the dish pit. But there really isn't any pattern at all. It’s completely random. I could be doing literally anything, anywhere in the restaurant. I could be wiping down the pie case, or filling the salt shakers, or just waiting by the swinging doors for Jimmy to put a plate up in the window. \n\nAnd I know... look, I know I am practically beating you over the head with this one specific story today, and I really apologize for bringing it up yet again, but it’s just the absolute best way I can explain why there's no pattern, and why it's so terrifying. Because what scared everyone at work so much wasn't *where* I was standing, it was the fact that I started freezing up right in the middle of service and just doing things completely, entirely out of order. \n\nIf there was a pattern, maybe it would make sense. But there wasn't. Like with that incident... the cash register is all the way up at the front host stand, right by the front door. But I was standing all the way over by the soda station. I was holding the cash drawer key, which meant my brain knew I was supposed to be up front closing out checks for my tables. But instead of doing that, instead of following the normal sequence of my job, I just got stuck by the ice machine, repeating those table numbers over and over again. It didn't make any sense for me to be doing that right then, or in that specific spot. I wasn't triggered by the soda machine, you know? It just... happened. It just hijacked me right there, completely out of order from what I was actually supposed to be doing, and that complete randomness is exactly why my manager was so freaked out by it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, if I really try to trace it all back, you know, to look at the whole timeline of when things started getting so incredibly difficult... it’s been about four months. It wasn't just a sudden thing where I woke up one Tuesday and started turning into a statue on the floor. It’s been building, because about four months ago is when the voices I hear started getting so much clearer. Like, incredibly distinct, to the point where I can't just tune them out or pretend they aren't there while I'm trying to memorize someone's breakfast order or refill their coffee. 

And it was right around that exact same time, four months ago, that I developed this... this absolute, firm belief that my coworkers and the customers coming into the diner were actually targeting me. 

And I know, look, I know I’m probably driving you crazy by bringing everything back to the restaurant and my shifts, and I really am trying to answer your question about the freezing, but you have to understand how it all connects for me. For the last four months, I’ve been walking into work every single day knowing—just completely certain—that the people sitting in my section and the guys working back on the grill are actively targeting me, all while these clearer voices are just constantly going in my head. It’s an enormous amount of pressure to be under when you're just trying to do a lunch rush. So the freezing up... it's like my system just overloads from all of it. So, yeah, to answer your question, it hasn't been just one random day, it's been this gradual escalation over the last four months since those voices got sharper and I finally realized what everyone at the diner was actually doing to me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Um, well, I guess my manager is the one who really pushed for this. I work as a waitress at this diner, and usually, I'm really good at my job. I went to college for a couple of years, so I'm smart, and I've been serving for a while now, and you know how it gets during a lunch rush—it's just go, go, go. You have to keep a million things in your head at once, the drinks, the modifications, who needs ranch. But lately... well, what really scared everyone at work, and honestly it kind of freaked me out too, is that I just... I started freezing up in the middle of service. \n\nLike, right in the middle of the floor. Or I'd start doing things completely out of order, which makes no sense because I know the routine backward and forward. The other day, my manager found me just standing over by the soda station. I was just standing there holding the key to the cash drawer, and instead of actually going to the register and closing out my checks like I was supposed to, I was just staring at the syrup boxes and repeating my table numbers out loud. Over and over. \"Table four, table seven, table twelve.\" Just like that. \n\nAnd my coworker had to come shake my shoulder because I guess I'd been doing it for a few minutes and the customers were waiting and the checks were just piling up. It's just... it's not like me to mess up the flow like that, but my body just stops, or my brain gets stuck on the numbers, and I can't get to the next step of what I'm supposed to be doing. They told me I needed to get checked out before I could come back on shift because it really freaked out the kitchen staff, too.\n\nDoctor: When you were standing at the soda station repeating the table numbers, were you aware of what was happening around you at that moment, or is it kind of a blank—like you don't remember what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I blacked out or anything. I wasn't unconscious, and it wasn't like a dream where you wake up and have no idea how you got there. I knew I was at the soda station. I could feel the cold air coming off the ice machine, and I could hear the clinking of the plates in the kitchen, and I knew the syrup boxes were right in front of me. The boxes were the ones with the little red labels, the cherry flavor, which we always run out of so fast, and I remember thinking about how we needed to change the bag soon. \n\nSo I was aware, yeah. I could hear the ambient noise of the diner, the low hum of people talking, the sizzle from the grill—Jimmy was working the grill that day, and he always makes this specific scraping sound with his spatula. But it was like all of that was happening on the other side of a thick glass window, you know? Like I was trapped in this little bubble where the only thing that mattered, the only thing that was real, was the table numbers. \"Table four, table seven, table twelve.\" \n\nI heard myself saying them, and I knew I was saying them out loud, but I just couldn't make myself stop and walk over to the register. It was like the numbers were the most important thing in the world, and if I didn't keep repeating them, something terrible would happen, or I'd just lose them forever. And I knew my hands were holding the key, the little brass key with the blue plastic tag that we use for the drawer, and I could feel the edges of it pressing into my palm, but my legs just wouldn't move. I was just stuck there, fully awake, fully aware that I was stuck, but completely unable to break out of the loop until Sarah—she's the coworker who came over—until she actually grabbed my shoulder and physically jolted me out of it.\n\nDoctor: When you were repeating those table numbers, did it feel like you absolutely had to do it because you were worried something bad would happen if you didn't, or did it feel more like your mind was just stuck on autopilot and you couldn't redirect your attention away from the numbers?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it’s definitely more like the autopilot thing, but... like a broken autopilot? Because, like I was trying to explain earlier, that’s exactly what scared everyone at work so much. It wasn’t that I was having some intense worry about the world ending or something terrible happening if I didn't say the numbers. It was just this total, complete inability to move to the next logical step. \n\nI just started freezing up right in the middle of service, you know? And doing things completely out of order. Like, I’ve got the cash drawer key right there in my hand. The only thing you do with that key is walk to the register, put it in, turn it, and close out the checks. That’s it. It’s a simple sequence, one I've done literally thousands of times since I got hired. But instead, my brain just got stuck on that one track, and I’m just standing there by the soda station, staring at the syrups, repeating those table numbers over and over instead of actually closing the checks. \n\nIt’s like the connection between knowing what I’m supposed to do and actually doing it just completely vanished. So I’m just frozen there, totally out of sequence, holding this key like it’s just some useless piece of metal, while the checks are just sitting there waiting to be closed. And I couldn't redirect my attention at all. No matter how much I knew I needed to go to the register, my mouth just kept going with the numbers. Which, you know, when you're in the middle of a lunch rush and the kitchen is backed up and everyone is relying on you to keep the flow going, freezing up like that and doing things so out of order is just... well, it's a disaster, really. It just makes no sense to my brain why I can't just walk over and use the key.\n\nDoctor: When you were repeating those table numbers, did it feel like something inside you was *pushing* you to keep saying them—like an urge or a compulsion you had to obey—or did it feel more like your body and mind just wouldn't respond to what you wanted to do, like they were stuck?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was definitely the second one. Like I was saying, it wasn't an urge at all. I didn't feel any kind of... push, or like I was being forced to obey some rule or anything like that. I know I keep coming back to this one specific incident, but it's just that this is exactly what scared everyone at work so badly, you know? Because I was just... stuck. Completely stuck. I wanted to move. I knew I needed to move. But I was just freezing up right in the middle of service. \n\nIt’s like my brain just forgot how to send the signals to my legs. I’m standing there by the soda station, which is right in the main walkway between the kitchen doors and the dining room, so literally everyone can see me, and I’m holding that cash drawer key. The logical next step, the thing I actively wanted to do, was to walk up to the front counter—it's right past the pie display—and close out the checks for those tables. That's the normal routine. But instead of closing the checks, I'm doing things completely out of order. I'm just frozen there, staring straight ahead at the syrups, and my mouth is just running on a loop, repeating those table numbers. \n\nI wasn't doing it because I felt a compulsion to do it. It was more like... I don't know, like a computer freezing on a single screen, and the audio just keeps skipping on the exact same word over and over. I wanted to break out of it, I wanted to just take the key and go to the register, but my body and mind just absolutely would not respond to what I was trying to make them do. And that’s why my manager and the kitchen staff were so freaked out, because standing by the soda machine reciting numbers instead of doing my job... it just doesn't make any sense. I was trapped in the glitch, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Before you started repeating those table numbers, did you notice any thoughts or worries going through your mind that felt intrusive or hard to push away—like thoughts that kept popping back into your head even though you didn't want them to? Or did the repetition of the numbers just start happening without any particular thoughts leading it?\n\nPatient: No, there really wasn't anything like that. I wasn't having any bad thoughts, or, you know, worries that I couldn't push away or anything. Honestly, right before it happened, my mind was just doing the normal diner calculus. It was the middle of the lunch rush, so I was just mentally mapping out my next steps, which is something you have to do when you're managing a whole section by yourself. \n\nI remember I had just left table twelve—they were this nice older couple who come in a lot, and they wanted the meatloaf special, but he's very particular about the gravy being on the side, so I was making a mental note to make sure Jimmy didn't smother it. And then table seven had just flagged me down for iced tea refills, and table four was ready to pay. So I was just organizing that sequence in my head. You know, get the check closed out for four, grab the tea pitcher for seven, put the ticket in for twelve. It’s just waitress math. My brain was completely focused on the logistics of the floor, just keeping the rhythm going. \n\nThere were no weird thoughts popping up, no anxiety about something going wrong, no intrusive worries at all. I was just doing my job. I walked over to the soda station because the extra tea pitchers are kept right next to the ice bin, and I was just reaching out to grab the handle—it’s this heavy plastic pitcher that always gets condensation all over the outside so you have to grip it tight—and that’s when the glitch just... happened. It just started out of nowhere. One second I'm thinking about iced tea and gravy, and the very next second, my brain just dropped everything else and the numbers took over and I was frozen. There was no build-up or warning or any specific thought leading into it at all, it just hijacked me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this freezing up has happened a few times at work. In those other episodes when you froze up, did you always find yourself repeating something out loud like you did with the table numbers, or have there been times when you just couldn't move or couldn't get your body to do what you wanted it to do without any repetitive behavior happening?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it’s not always the talking. I know I’ve been talking your ear off about that one specific time with the table numbers today, but I just want to make sure you really understand the core of why it was such a big deal. Because what genuinely scared everyone at work wasn't necessarily just the repeating out loud part—it's the fact that I started freezing up right in the middle of service. Period. Just stopping dead in my tracks. Or doing things completely out of order, which, in a restaurant, is basically a cardinal sin, you know? \n\nYou have a system, you follow the system. Everything is about timing and sequence. So when I break that system, people notice immediately. Like, I know I already brought up standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key instead of actually going to the register and closing out the checks, but that’s just the absolute perfect example of doing things out of order. The repeating table numbers part of that specific incident was terrifying, sure, but there have definitely been other times where I just freeze and I'm completely silent. \n\nI won't be saying a single word. I'll just be standing there, totally stuck, maybe holding a tray or a coffee pot or even that same cash drawer key, and my body just won't go. It's the freezing and the out-of-sequence actions that really throw everyone off. Because one minute I'm fine, walking through my section, and the next minute I'm just a statue in the middle of the main aisle, not doing the very obvious next thing I'm supposed to be doing. And the whole kitchen staff and my manager are just staring at me, wondering why I'm not moving when there are tickets piling up. So, yeah, to answer your question, the verbal repeating part isn't always there, but the freezing up and the doing things out of order... that's the constant. That's the part that really shook up the staff.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up like that, is it always something that just happens to you without warning, or have you noticed that it tends to happen more when you're feeling stressed or anxious about something specific—either at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, it really isn’t about being stressed or anxious. I mean, sure, working in a diner during the lunch rush is inherently stressful, right? You’ve got plates clattering, the line cooks are yelling because they need hands to run food, people are waving their empty coffee mugs at you, the phone is ringing for takeout orders... it’s a high-pressure environment. But that’s just the job. I know how to handle a rush. It’s a good kind of stress, usually, like a workout. And my personal life is pretty quiet right now, there’s no big drama with my family or anything keeping me up at night. So it’s not like I’m standing on the floor worrying about my bills or having a panic attack about my life. It really just happens completely without warning. \n\nI know I keep circling back to this, and I probably sound like a broken record today, but I just really need to make sure you see the whole picture of why this was such a massive deal to the people I work with. Because what actually scared everyone at work so much wasn't that I was acting stressed out or overwhelmed—it was that I started freezing up right in the absolute middle of service, or just doing things completely out of order for no logical reason. \n\nLike that incident I keep talking about... standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and just repeating those table numbers over and over instead of actually going up to the front and closing out the checks. If I was just stressed, I might snap at a busboy or, I don't know, forget a side of ranch or drop a plate. But freezing up and doing my routine entirely out of order like that? Standing by the syrups with the register key instead of cashing people out? That’s not anxiety. That’s just... my brain completely losing the plot without any warning whatsoever. And that’s why my manager and Jimmy in the kitchen looked at me like I had two heads, because I wasn't acting frantic or anxious, I was just completely broken out of the normal sequence of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up like that, are you able to move other parts of your body—like your arms or your head—or does your entire body just go completely rigid and locked in place?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s… it’s like my whole body just turns to concrete. I really can’t move my arms or even just turn my head to look at someone if they yell my name. I’m completely locked in place. And I know, I know I’ve brought this specific example up like five times already today, and I'm sorry to keep harping on it, but it’s just… it’s the exact reason why everyone at the diner got so incredibly scared. Because it’s not just that I get a little stiff or need a second to catch my breath, it’s the freezing up right in the absolute middle of a crazy service, and doing everything completely out of order. \n\nLike, when I was stuck there by the soda station, my arm was just… suspended. I was holding that cash drawer key, and my fingers were clamped around it so tight my knuckles were probably white, but I couldn't lower my arm or walk to the front. I couldn't make my legs move toward the register to actually close out the checks like I was supposed to. I was just a statue. The only thing moving was my mouth, just repeating those table numbers over and over, while the rest of me was just totally rigid. I couldn't even turn my neck to look at my manager when he came over to see what was wrong. So, yeah, it’s the whole body. It just locks up completely, and I'm stuck there doing things entirely out of sequence instead of my actual job.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when your whole body freezes up, are you completely conscious and aware of everything happening around you the whole time, or do you lose consciousness or have gaps in your memory of what happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, I am completely awake. That’s… honestly, that’s the absolute worst part of the whole thing. I don't black out at all, not even for a second. I remember every single detail. I can hear the plates clattering in the bus tubs, I can hear the bell dinging in the window when food is up, I can smell the fryer grease... my brain is just sitting there inside my head watching everything happen, but I just can't do anything about it. \n\nAnd I know, look, I know I keep coming back to this same point, and I swear I'm not trying to beat a dead horse today, but the fact that I am totally awake and aware is exactly what scared everyone at work so badly. Because they could look at my face and see that my eyes were open and tracking things while I started freezing up right in the middle of service, or doing things entirely out of order. They could tell I wasn't fainting or having a seizure where I was checked out. \n\nLike, during that time I keep talking about, when I was standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key? I knew I was holding it. I knew exactly what it was for, and I knew in my head that I was supposed to be walking up to the front register to actually close out those checks. But instead, I'm just planted there, repeating those table numbers out loud over and over, and I'm fully, one-hundred-percent aware that I'm doing it instead of closing the checks! I could literally see my manager walking over looking incredibly confused, I could hear a customer in my section asking for a side of mayo, but I just couldn't stop the repeating or make my legs move away from the ice machine. \n\nSo, no, there are absolutely no gaps in my memory. None. I just have to stand there, completely trapped inside my own body, knowing that I'm breaking the entire flow of the restaurant and terrifying my coworkers because I'm not doing the next logical step in my job. It’s just… it’s really exhausting to remember it all so clearly, to be perfectly honest with you, because I know exactly how bizarre it looks to everyone else while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at all the times you've frozen up at work, have you noticed any kind of pattern—like it happens more when you're in a particular area of the diner, or when you're doing a specific task, or around certain people? Or does it really just happen completely randomly no matter what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: I mean... I’ve tried to figure that out, honestly. I’ve racked my brain trying to see if there’s some sort of common denominator, like maybe it only happens when I’m dealing with a difficult customer at booth four, or maybe it’s when I’m carrying the heavy bus tubs back to the dish pit. But there really isn't any pattern at all. It’s completely random. I could be doing literally anything, anywhere in the restaurant. I could be wiping down the pie case, or filling the salt shakers, or just waiting by the swinging doors for Jimmy to put a plate up in the window. \n\nAnd I know... look, I know I am practically beating you over the head with this one specific story today, and I really apologize for bringing it up yet again, but it’s just the absolute best way I can explain why there's no pattern, and why it's so terrifying. Because what scared everyone at work so much wasn't *where* I was standing, it was the fact that I started freezing up right in the middle of service and just doing things completely, entirely out of order. \n\nIf there was a pattern, maybe it would make sense. But there wasn't. Like with that incident... the cash register is all the way up at the front host stand, right by the front door. But I was standing all the way over by the soda station. I was holding the cash drawer key, which meant my brain knew I was supposed to be up front closing out checks for my tables. But instead of doing that, instead of following the normal sequence of my job, I just got stuck by the ice machine, repeating those table numbers over and over again. It didn't make any sense for me to be doing that right then, or in that specific spot. I wasn't triggered by the soda machine, you know? It just... happened. It just hijacked me right there, completely out of order from what I was actually supposed to be doing, and that complete randomness is exactly why my manager was so freaked out by it.\n\nDoctor: How long have these freezing episodes been happening? Did they start suddenly one day, or have they been gradually getting worse over weeks or months?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really try to trace it all back, you know, to look at the whole timeline of when things started getting so incredibly difficult... it’s been about four months. It wasn't just a sudden thing where I woke up one Tuesday and started turning into a statue on the floor. It’s been building, because about four months ago is when the voices I hear started getting so much clearer. Like, incredibly distinct, to the point where I can't just tune them out or pretend they aren't there while I'm trying to memorize someone's breakfast order or refill their coffee. \n\nAnd it was right around that exact same time, four months ago, that I developed this... this absolute, firm belief that my coworkers and the customers coming into the diner were actually targeting me. \n\nAnd I know, look, I know I’m probably driving you crazy by bringing everything back to the restaurant and my shifts, and I really am trying to answer your question about the freezing, but you have to understand how it all connects for me. For the last four months, I’ve been walking into work every single day knowing—just completely certain—that the people sitting in my section and the guys working back on the grill are actively targeting me, all while these clearer voices are just constantly going in my head. It’s an enormous amount of pressure to be under when you're just trying to do a lunch rush. So the freezing up... it's like my system just overloads from all of it. So, yeah, to answer your question, it hasn't been just one random day, it's been this gradual escalation over the last four months since those voices got sharper and I finally realized what everyone at the diner was actually doing to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

These psychotic features rule out OCD (F42.9) and point toward primary psychotic disorders. The freezing episodes and repetitive behaviors appear secondary to psychotic symptoms rather than primary obsessive-compulsive phenomena.",
  "is_final": false

Because it’s not just that I get a little stiff or need a second to catch my breath, it’s the freezing up right in the absolute middle of a crazy service, and doing everything completely out of order. \n\nLike, when I was stuck there by the soda station, my arm was just… suspended. I was holding that cash drawer key, and my fingers were clamped around it so tight my knuckles were probably white, but I couldn't lower my arm or walk to the front. I couldn't make my legs move toward the register to actually close out the checks like I was supposed to. I was just a statue. The only thing moving was my mouth, just repeating those table numbers over and over, while the rest of me was just totally rigid. I couldn't even turn my neck to look at my manager when he came over to see what was wrong. So, yeah, it’s the whole body. It just locks up completely, and I'm stuck there doing things entirely out of sequence instead of my actual job.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when your whole body freezes up, are you completely conscious and aware of everything happening around you the whole time, or do you lose consciousness or have gaps in your memory of what happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, I am completely awake. That’s… honestly, that’s the absolute worst part of the whole thing. I don't black out at all, not even for a second. I remember every single detail. I can hear the plates clattering in the bus tubs, I can hear the bell dinging in the window when food is up, I can smell the fryer grease... my brain is just sitting there inside my head watching everything happen, but I just can't do anything about it. \n\nAnd I know, look, I know I keep coming back to this same point, and I swear I'm not trying to beat a dead horse today, but the fact that I am totally awake and aware is exactly what scared everyone at work so badly. Because they could look at my face and see that my eyes were open and tracking things while I started freezing up right in the middle of service, or doing things entirely out of order. They could tell I wasn't fainting or having a seizure where I was checked out. \n\nLike, during that time I keep talking about, when I was standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key? I knew I was holding it. I knew exactly what it was for, and I knew in my head that I was supposed to be walking up to the front register to actually close out those checks. But instead, I'm just planted there, repeating those table numbers out loud over and over, and I'm fully, one-hundred-percent aware that I'm doing it instead of closing the checks! I could literally see my manager walking over looking incredibly confused, I could hear a customer in my section asking for a side of mayo, but I just couldn't stop the repeating or make my legs move away from the ice machine. \n\nSo, no, there are absolutely no gaps in my memory. None. I just have to stand there, completely trapped inside my own body, knowing that I'm breaking the entire flow of the restaurant and terrifying my coworkers because I'm not doing the next logical step in my job. It’s just… it’s really exhausting to remember it all so clearly, to be perfectly honest with you, because I know exactly how bizarre it looks to everyone else while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at all the times you've frozen up at work, have you noticed any kind of pattern—like it happens more when you're in a particular area of the diner, or when you're doing a specific task, or around certain people? Or does it really just happen completely randomly no matter what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: I mean... I’ve tried to figure that out, honestly. I’ve racked my brain trying to see if there’s some sort of common denominator, like maybe it only happens when I’m dealing with a difficult customer at booth four, or maybe it’s when I’m carrying the heavy bus tubs back to the dish pit. But there really isn't any pattern at all. It’s completely random. I could be doing literally anything, anywhere in the restaurant. I could be wiping down the pie case, or filling the salt shakers, or just waiting by the swinging doors for Jimmy to put a plate up in the window. \n\nAnd I know... look, I know I am practically beating you over the head with this one specific story today, and I really apologize for bringing it up yet again, but it’s just the absolute best way I can explain why there's no pattern, and why it's so terrifying. Because what scared everyone at work so much wasn't *where* I was standing, it was the fact that I started freezing up right in the middle of service and just doing things completely, entirely out of order. \n\nIf there was a pattern, maybe it would make sense. But there wasn't. Like with that incident... the cash register is all the way up at the front host stand, right by the front door. But I was standing all the way over by the soda station. I was holding the cash drawer key, which meant my brain knew I was supposed to be up front closing out checks for my tables. But instead of doing that, instead of following the normal sequence of my job, I just got stuck by the ice machine, repeating those table numbers over and over again. It didn't make any sense for me to be doing that right then, or in that specific spot. I wasn't triggered by the soda machine, you know? It just... happened. It just hijacked me right there, completely out of order from what I was actually supposed to be doing, and that complete randomness is exactly why my manager was so freaked out by it.\n\nDoctor: How long have these freezing episodes been happening? Did they start suddenly one day, or have they been gradually getting worse over weeks or months?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really try to trace it all back, you know, to look at the whole timeline of when things started getting so incredibly difficult... it’s been about four months. It wasn't just a sudden thing where I woke up one Tuesday and started turning into a statue on the floor. It’s been building, because about four months ago is when the voices I hear started getting so much clearer. Like, incredibly distinct, to the point where I can't just tune them out or pretend they aren't there while I'm trying to memorize someone's breakfast order or refill their coffee. \n\nAnd it was right around that exact same time, four months ago, that I developed this... this absolute, firm belief that my coworkers and the customers coming into the diner were actually targeting me. \n\nAnd I know, look, I know I’m probably driving you crazy by bringing everything back to the restaurant and my shifts, and I really am trying to answer your question about the freezing, but you have to understand how it all connects for me. For the last four months, I’ve been walking into work every single day knowing—just completely certain—that the people sitting in my section and the guys working back on the grill are actively targeting me, all while these clearer voices are just constantly going in my head. It’s an enormous amount of pressure to be under when you're just trying to do a lunch rush. So the freezing up... it's like my system just overloads from all of it. So, yeah, to answer your question, it hasn't been just one random day, it's been this gradual escalation over the last four months since those voices got sharper and I finally realized what everyone at the diner was actually doing to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s not really like they’re ordering me around like a drill sergeant or anything like that. It’s... well, it's a woman’s voice. And she usually pipes up when things are quiet, like during the closing shift. You know, when the open sign is finally turned off, the front doors are locked, the bus tubs are all washed out, and I’m in the back trying to do the drawer count. 

The count is already so stressful on its own because if you're off by even a couple of dollars, management throws an absolute fit, so I'm already trying to focus really hard on matching the credit card slips to the cash. And right then, while I'm counting the fives and twenties, she’ll just... say my name. Or she’ll tell me I’m doing the count wrong. Just out of nowhere, she'll tell me I'm messing up the math. 

And it’s not even just at work, either. It happens when I’m home, too, usually when I’m in my own kitchen. I’ll just be standing there, maybe getting a glass of water or wiping down my own counters after a long shift, and I'll hear her. And doctor, I cannot even begin to explain to you how incredibly clear she sounds. It is so crisp and real, it doesn't sound like a thought in my head, it sounds exactly like there is a physical human being standing right behind my shoulder. 

It’s so clear that I actually answer her! I’ll literally spin around and say out loud, "What?" or "No I'm not, I just counted the tens!" And then... I just stop. Because I look around my kitchen, or the back office at the diner, and I realize I'm completely alone. Nobody else heard it because nobody else is there. But I already answered her out loud before my brain could even process that she wasn't real. It’s just so exhausting to deal with, having to constantly second-guess if someone is actually standing right behind me or not, especially when I'm just trying to finish up my night and go to bed.
